ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e205	I want to start by thanking the Committee for a good and thorough work on these two issues. We have dealt with the distributions – and I would like to add – of the very good statements by the Foreign Minister and the Minister for Defence, and we have addressed the representative proposal from Red, which largely addresses many of the same questions, but with some additional points. These are very important things. There are demanding questions when Norway decides to participate in international operations — yes, when Norway decides whether we should participate in war. That is why there is an important debate on how we can best ensure proper decisions, and on how our processes with maximum openness guarantee quality and hence legitimacy for the decisions being taken. There is a clear division of responsibilities between accumulation and government linked to Norwegian participation in international operations. A clear multiple thinks it's right that that division of responsibilities is fixed. It is the government that is the executive, which determines any Norwegian participation in international operations. No decision is required from the Storing page. The majority also believe it is important that a real consultation be conducted with the Stortinget, but the same plurality at the same time underlines that it does not take full responsibility from the government for the decision. However, I would like to stress the positive and long practice of different governments to ensure that a decisive emphasis on broad support is placed on key foreign and security policy issues. It was the decision of the Stortinget on 18 April 2017 that invited the government to take the initiative to carry out an evaluation in its own form of the Norwegian military and civil participation in the Libyan operation in 2011. The Committee believes that a very thorough and good job has been done in the preparation of the report – Evaluation of Norwegian participation in the Libyan operations in 2011. It's a very good and useful report the Petersen committee has delivered. The Petersen report provides a thorough review of decision-making processes and a holistic evaluation of Norwegian participation in Libya operations in 2011. The report provides important guidance and experience for future Norwegian contributions. The report contributes to a better and more informed public debate on Norwegian foreign and security policy. It is correct and important that operations of the same type as the Libya operations are evaluated and reviewed. This could lead to improvements for future operations of this type and increase confidence in the population. Although the decision on Norwegian participation in Libya operations in 2011 was taken in a very short time, consultations were conducted against Stortinget. In the subsequent public reactions, however, there was uncertainty as to whether the government had acted constructively correctly in relation to the Storting. One question in the public debate was whether the consultation was carried out in time. The Committee believes that a good consultation mechanism would provide greater confidence in the population of critical and important decisions about participation in international operations. At the same time, the Committee would stress that the selection concludes that the formal processes leading to the decision to participate with Norwegian forces met all the constitutional requirements, even though the government’s decision to participate was taken in a very short time. The Committee also points out that the selection clearly confirms that the Norwegian contribution for the strength complied with Norway’s humanitarian obligations. It is clear that the government assumed that the UN Security Council resolution was an adequate basis for international law for the use of military power in Libya. The Committee has noted that no written assessments were given to the government as to the framework of the Security Council Resolution for the use of military power. The Committee would point out that the lack of just written evidence makes the control task of the Storting difficult in retrospect and may lead to a lack of confidence in the decision-making base of the population. This will be an important debate. For my part, I will also take the word later. In the setting, there are different ratings, not least from the SV party, for which they will do well in the debate.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e226	Let me first acknowledge the work of the Petersen Committee. We have received a thorough review of the Norwegian military and civil contributions to the Libyan operation as well as the national decision-making processes underlying them. I also agree with the main conclusions of a broad majority in the position. As Minister of Foreign Affairs of the Government of Norway during the Libya operation, I had a special responsibility. As head of the Workers' Party, I wanted – and supported – to get a review of this operation. It should be the rule and not the exception that we go through military operations we've been involved in, to draw lessons from them. I read in some comments that responsible politicians have not learned from Libya. Defender Tormod Heier says in an interview to the Class Fight on March 28th: "As Jens Stoltenberg as Jonas Gahr Støre and Espen Barth Eide have said they would do the same again." It's a claim based on a basic misunderstanding that it's worth clarifying. What I've explained, in the spring of 2011, a number of times since and in my answers to the Petersen Committee, is: why we acted the way we did how we read the situation in 2011, as the conflict in Libya drew attention to the way in which we and large parts of the world society, including Libya's neighbours in Africa, considered that there was a possible acute threat of assault against civilians in Libya by a dictator's military forces the importance we attach to the various decisions of the UN Security Council until the decisive resolution, which authorized the use of all available means – read: military power to prevent abuses And I explained the experience our generation had from past situations where the world community did not act adequately, as in Srebrenica and Rwanda in the 1990s. I am in favour of what we, a collective government, have decided, with the support of an overall collapse. I believe Norwegian forces, mainly from the Air Force, carried out their mission in a good way and deserve our recognition. Then I recognize that Libya has been in a critically unstable situation after Gaddafi's fall, and that a number of critical questions can and should be asked about the follow-up of the international community after that. It's also important lessons to learn from how the operation was managed and coordinated. This is also done by the Committee in its report. I think it is important lessons that Norway brings with it when we participate in international debates, in NATO and in the UN, on how we manage international operations. The question if we would do the same thing again, doesn't make us wise, because March 2011 won't come back. It's been. But the experience we gained before, during and after the Libyan operation, we obviously draw with ourselves – the findings made by the Petersen Committee and the treatment here in Stortinget as well. The meaning of a review is, of course, to draw lessons that can make us better equipped to assess, manage and decide in a critical situation in the future. This shows the option. For the Workers' Party, decisions of the UN Security Council will always weigh heavily when considering our possible involvement. The basis of international law must be solid. When the Security Council asks the world community to take responsibility, Norway should always consider how we can contribute, because we are so dependent on a regulated world order where the United Nations plays a central role. I would warn those who say that Norway is about to refuse to participate in international operations on a principle basis. Norway has its NATO obligations. We place great emphasis on decisions in the UN Security Council. Our security depends in many respects on the willingness of other countries to engage in international operations to secure Norway and the north. As a result, Norway should be open for its part to take responsibility beyond our borders — once again, of course, on a safe basis of international law. We cannot and should not therefore provide a categorical response to where Norway should open up to engage its military forces. Of course, we have to weigh heavily what we need to ensure ongoing military preparedness for Norway’s security. It is increasingly happening here in Norway and in our neighbourhoods, in the light of the current security policy situation. When it comes to participating in international operations, we should carefully assess the areas in which we have capacities that are both relevant and available. This should be part of the ongoing debate on society, including here in the Stortinget. As for the views of the Workers' Party on the other parts of the adjustment, I leave them to other speakers from our party to refer to them.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e293	Norway’s participation in the Libyan military operation in 2011 raises a number of fundamental, constitutional and moral issues. Various aspects of these issues are raised in the report of the Petersen Committee and later by statements by both the Minister of Foreign Affairs and the Minister of Defence. With this as a backstop, there is a lot of will to find that the government is not taking these issues seriously. While the selection and presentations were full, clear and in principle, the comments submitted are a good example of the opposite. The story presented is long and detailed, but is primarily a masterpiece of disclaimer. The desire to continue in government was greater than the moral indignation. The truth is that a total increase joined Norway's war effort in Libya. Then we can talk later about premises, mandates, purposes, decision-making processes and consequences. But let's make it clear: we were all involved. We also know what conditions are like in Libya today. Despite an isolated and successful military operation, the war in Libya does not leave a country and people with renewed hope for the future. That in itself is a lesson that we should all take on. All parties agreed that Norway would contribute to the war in order to protect civilians. Whether this was a deposit from some of our allies, and that the goal was actually a regime change, it is not possible to get full clarity today. The Petersen Committee's report also concludes that the goal of the military operation was never a regime change. You can ask yourself the question of whether it is possible at all to bomb a country in order to secure civilians, but it was in any case a common hope we had. However, the issue becomes even more problematic when some today find it morally reprehensible that Norwegian bombing may have led to the loss of civil life. SV, in its observations, conducts a manifest form of double moralism. The Party supports bombing in order to protect civilians if it has broad international support and mandate from the UN but finds the war irresponsible and illegal when the war changes its objective. At the same time, SV must ask himself how the war in Libya could have ended with Gaddafi still at the rudder. One would like to discuss the pope's beard, but realistically it would be an impossibility if Gaddafi had continued to rule Libya, and no one seriously believes that Gaddafi had stepped aside from his own free will. SV's very long and comprehensive remark, I would assume, is due to the fact that the party has some idealistic notion of warfare. SV writes that they initially supported Norwegian participation, with clear reservations that the purpose was the protection of civil rather than regime change. Even pacifists should understand that military operations in war are becoming incomprehensible and that the elections you make may have unintentioned consequences. In the military theory, the inaccuracy of war is called the "fog of war". War operations are not a desktop practice or a chess tournament. Even if you're not initially working for a regime change, it could be the result of a military operation. The aim was, of course, to weaken the regime's capacity to attack civilians, thus giving the armed opposition a clear advantage. This eventually led to the fall of the regime and the death of Gaddafi. The government was responsible for the decision in 2011, but Stortinget was consulted in line with the Basic Law. At the same time, let's say that the government put the Workers' Party, the Center Party and SV. Apart from the fact that there should be parties in government other than that at the time, the future responsibility should also lie with the executive. It's strange to observe that parts of the opposition, as was then part of the government, seem to distance themselves from the decisions they themselves agreed to make. It may not be that a government is responsible only when it goes according to plan, but that it two hands if it does not go as you had imagined. Our soldiers must know that they have the government behind them at all times. They don't go to war on behalf of themselves, but on behalf of the state power. SV also points out in its comments that there was a lack of situational understanding before the decision to contribute militarily. I fully agree that good and defamatory information is necessary. But in a conflict situation, there will always be a lack of information. There will always be factors that play in, that you haven't thought of, and there will always be a danger that the situation will evolve in a completely different direction than you predicted. To think that you want a situation with enough information is to live in a dream world. It is important to learn from this and other military operations that Norway participates in. Experience gained through participation in foreign missions should provide a better basis for decision-making and strengthen the reflection on the impact of this type of effort both politically in Norway and in the area affected by the operation. At the same time, it is necessary to take time to assess the operations to be undertaken by Norway. As a NATO member, of course, Article 5 operations must involve Norwegian involvement. But it's not as obvious that we're going to participate in any UN-led operation. Norwegian resources are limited, and Norwegian influence is actually minimal in operations managed by major actors. Therefore, it should be assessed on a case-by-case basis whether it is in Norway’s interest to use resources on some types of UN mission. It must also be perfectly legitimate and, in fact, absolutely necessary to ask the question of whether Norwegian military action has led to something positive. It's one thing to win the war, but it's far more demanding to win the peace. And it must be legitimate to ask ourselves whether our allies at all times have the same motives for their participation in the war as we allegedly have. SV shows with its comments that the party has an opposite approach. The Party wishes to contribute to UN-led missions. However, the fight against the Taliban in Afghanistan did not want the party to join, despite the fact that it was enshrined in Article 5, which states that an attack on one is to be seen as an attack on all. In 2001, SV proposed that Norwegian forces should not be put at the disposal of military operations in Afghanistan under the command of the United States. The SV leader of Kristin Halvorsen, according to Dagbladet, stated in the big debate on the strength contribution to Afghanistan in 2001, that SV would support the participation of Norwegian military forces in a UN-led security and peace force. To the reprinted Thorbjørn Jagland: ‘I would like to ask whether SV has thought through what would be the consequences if SV’s view were also to become Norway’s official policy.’ He did not get an answer to the question. I am therefore repeating the question in some other form here: would SV have called upon the UN if one of the 29 member states of NATO had been attacked militarily or through state-supported terror? Any Norwegian participation in a military operation outside NATO must be assessed from different perspectives. However, a decision-making process on military participation cannot be seen as a seminar. There are several good reasons for this. In reality, it will be impossible for the government to give the Stortinget decision-making authority over Norwegian military participation. Such a decision will always be based on highly classified information – information received by allies in confidentiality, information which they will not be able to share with the Storting until, if necessary, at a later date. A debate based on up-to-date information will be meaningless. The fact that SV stands for such a view is not surprising, but that the centre party has taken the same position is alarming. Not only does the Central Party claim that the decision should be taken in Stortinget, but it must even be taken in the case of the release of the representatives. The Central Party will establish a war bomb in which each single representative of the Grand Duchy will vote as he or she sees fit. We can then, for example, have a decision in which the Stortinget, by 85 to 84 votes, decides that Norway should go to war. I would like to meet the general who had put his soldiers' lives at risk with such a lack of support and solidarity in his back.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e362	I would like to thank you for the presentations and for the comprehensive report of the sample. There were serious reasons for saying yes to Norwegian participation in Libya in the spring of 2011. The protests spread in the Middle East this winter – finally a hope of democracy, a hope that today has largely turned to hopelessness, war and oppression. Also in Libya people took to the streets, but the Libyan regime's response was violent and cash, and the UN reports showed systematic attacks on the civilian population. The threat of assault, which was described, was perceived as real and acute. So the 1973 Security Council Resolution was the first of its kind. Never before had the international community decided to use military power to protect civilians. This was behind Norway’s decision to participate — to protect the civilian population in Libya from what the UN warned against becoming a massacre, and to end up around the UN. The position of the SV and the Norwegian government was clear: it was not meant to be a war for regime change, but a no-fly zone to protect civilians. Any regime change would be the work of the Libyan people and not be created from the outside. The war evolved in a different direction. Despite the starting point — a mandate to protect civilians — and despite the fact that Norway, as the report also concludes, never had a regime change as a target, this did not vote for the largest of Norway’s allies. The aim of the powers of the storms became increasingly clear: they wanted to change the regime. The mandate was, in our opinion, broken through this. The support was also missing. The Arab League turned around. The African Union was critical. Therefore, early in May 2011, SV joined in order to complete the Norwegian mission. The government subsequently took such a decision in the summer of 2011. It was correct and important that Norway withdrew at that time. We have to learn from the Libyan War. The report and the statements are of no value if we do not take up our experience and change our policies on the points that are necessary for the future. One of the things that experience shows us is that NATO is a better tool for the major-est interests than it is for the United Nations. The term of office was used differently from that initially intended. This is also the reason why SV has since gone against participation in U.S.-led coalitions in the Middle East. Sending soldiers to war is a serious decision. The sample concludes that it happened within the constitutional framework, but it happened quickly. A rapid process of low publicity and debate creates uncertainty and weakens public confidence. SV therefore suggests – as we have done before – that the government’s reason for participation in any future military operation must be presented to the Stortinget and dealt with through voting in the Stortinget. The committee also recommends that Norway develop a so-called checklist for this type of decision. We agree with this and have proposed in the setting a number of points that should be on such a list. The committee concludes that there are no points of reference to claim that Norway has broken our humanitarian obligations. Nevertheless, I believe that it is essential to strive for as much openness as possible around that question. In Denmark, information on the tokens was updated on a daily basis. I believe that we now have to do the same in Norway, and we therefore propose that the government publish all relevant information on the bombings in Norway. As regards Document 8:68, we agree that the Constitution § 25 and 26 should be revised. However, it is the Stortinge that adopts constitutional changes, so the proposal must come from Stortinge. SV will make such a proposal. Today we see a locked situation in the world and in the UN where it is unmistakable that the major powers will be able to gather for a mandate to protect civilians again. This is a result of how the Western powers handled the UN mandate during the Libyan War. The SV proposal will help Norway to learn from what went wrong, and that this exercise will not be a symbolic act, but a debate in which we really take lessons and change Norwegian policy. I would like to include in this the range of suggestions that SV is left alone in the setting.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e397	‘The Norwegian F16 aircraft and their crews did during the war in Libya were extremely violent, devastating and deadly. Norway has not used more brutal military power since World War II.» These aren't my words, this is VG's description of the nearly 600 bombs that Norwegian planes dropped over Libya in NATO's war of regime change in 2011. The then government, the Stoltenberg government, claimed that the bombings against Libya were to protect the country's civilians. The result was the opposite. Altogether, millions of Libyans were injured, traumatized, killed or driven away, and Libya was a haven for Islamic terrorist groups like Al Qaida and IS just outside Europe's living door. From the British Parliament's investigation, we also know that the intervention was built on manifestly questionable intelligence, propaganda and disinformation, and that our NATO allies were always intent on breaking the UN mandate by bombing regime change. Never since World War II has a Norwegian government decision had such tragic consequences for as many human lives as the decision to bomb Libya. All of this is known by the elected representatives of the Grand Duchy, who are today going to vote on the settlement of the Libyan war. That's why it's disappointing, we think, that the parties in the Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence reject our proposal for what should be the obvious lesson of the Libyan war: that the Norwegian Community's defence resources should be used to defend Norway, not to participate in the dubious attacks of other major powers on countries that do not threaten us or any allies of us. One reason why Norway is a quite well-functioning society, with little corruption and great transparency, is that in Norway we have strict laws, rules and procedures that regulate the rulers and that we take decisions on the right factual basis, that policy-makers have to take responsibility when things go wrong, and that mistakes are corrected and prevented for the future, with new decisions, laws and rules. Clearly, it has a preventative effect when politicians know that they can have their political career ruined because of things like formal flaws in the construction of a jetty or similar. But when it comes to decisions about war and peace, these rules don't apply to taking responsibility and correcting mistakes. 20 years ago, Norway bombed Yugoslavia without a UN mandate. The war in Afghanistan was, according to the Godal Committee, a total failure, and Libya became even worse. But no matter how huge the gap between the promises made by politicians when they want to go to war, and the results of the war lead to, nothing happens. No one should take responsibility, and no corrections are made. It also shows the positions of the Foreign Affairs and Defence Committee. Despite the fact that Stoltenberg's error of the Storting was quite obvious, it was only Red who did something to make him explain himself to the Storting. Despite Amnesty International saying that a number of civilians were killed and injured in NATO's bombing, only Red will have an independent investigation of how many were killed in the Norwegian attacks. And despite the total failure of the Libyan war, like the Afghanistan war, it is only Red that will decide that the Community's tax money and Norwegian soldiers' lives should not be wasted on the war adventures of other major powers, but used to defend our country. That's why there's also a bigger and more fundamental question we're going to deal with today. Will the most serious decisions on war and peace continue to be exempt from all normal principles of responsibility and correction of errors, or will the use of Norwegian military force for assault wars on false premises and with devastating results for millions of innocents lead to dishwashing and course change, which occurs in the event of serious errors and misdeeds in all other policy areas? With this I take up Red's proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e418	The representative Bears Moxnes has taken up the suggestions he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e424	First, I would like to thank the case rapporteur for a good job with the setting. I would also like to thank the sample who has submitted the report once again, and I would also like to refer to the statement I made in Stortinget on 8 January. The report to the Libya Committee has created a debate. There was also one of the objectives of the sample. Open debate on foreign and security policy choices and dilemmas is important and healthy. As I showed in my presentation, the circumstances surrounding the Libya operation were in many ways unique. There was a clear and broad mandate from the United Nations Security Council for military use under Chapter 7 of the United Nations Charter. Then the Arab League had asked for a military intervention in Libya. At home, our participation had the support of all the parties represented on the Stortinget, and the prevailing perception was that there was a great risk of a massacre on civilians if Gaddafi's forces were attacked by Benghazi, and that the international community had the opportunity to prevent the serious abuses. Norway, along with many others, responded to a clear call from the United Nations Security Council, and so the representative Moxnes describes as ‘the war adventure of the Great Powers’ is not correct. If we are not going to be able to respond to the clear demands of international society to intervene, in my opinion, our role in the world is quite different from that of a large majority here in the Chamber. Norway's participation in the operation was aimed at contributing to the fulfilment of the Security Council Resolution 1973, which authorized the use of power to protect the population and populated places, including Benghazi, and to implement the aviation ban zone over Libya. Libya, for its part, was obliged to comply with the Security Council's requirements, such as: immediately halted all acts of violence in Libya, immediate ceasefire and stop in all attacks on civilians. I notice that several people have asked questions about the situation of both the Security Council and Norway. There are different opinions internationally about what really happened on the ground in Libya in March 2011, and what intentions the Gaddafi regime had. But counter-factual discussions are little fruitful, and much of what you know – or think you know – today was not part of the 2011 decision-making base, either for the Security Council or for the government at that time. It would be more appropriate to discuss the various dilemmas associated with the use of military power, and I mentioned in particular four dilemmas in my presentation. One is to act versus not to act; the other is the question of what is good enough information; the third is the question of how a conflict and thus the military operation develops; and the fourth is the role of Norway within a broader international effort. These are the dilemmas to which those who always sit and rule must take an active stand. It is not an alternative to refrain from dealing with these questions. Nor is it possible to eliminate all risks in international operations. You never want perfect action options or full information. It is important for the government to stress that many of the recommendations contained in the Petersen Committee's report have already been followed up by the government. Let me use this as a clear example: in connection with Norwegian participation in the anti-ISIL coalition in Syria in 2016, the assessments of the international legal basis were published in writing and published in full. I have noticed that the Committee's position calls for this to be a future practice, and that the sample also believes that this is a good example of follow-up. This is also the government's clear intention, and that's why we did it. The aim of such publication was, of course, to ensure greater transparency in the assessments of this type of key decision and difficult and important decision making. It is important for confidence in the decisions, and it is also important to recognise, as the Committee also does in its position, that everything cannot be open and public, and that is why we have the consultation mechanisms of the enlarged Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence, which work well and as intended. As regards the issue of constitutional changes, this is also a point mentioned by the sample. I have noticed that, as such, naturally, the Committee does not come up with any clear recommendation, but points out some dangers, and in this context I will concentrate on so far as I agree with what the representative Lysbakken said, namely that it is the Storting that in that case must initiate constitutional amendments. And of course, there is a debate and a discussion that the government will then follow and, of course, follow – if there are any changes.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e663	This open debate is important in order to create an open debate on Norway’s participation in the Libyan operation and on other foreign operations. We had a debate in this room in 2011. We have agreed to evaluate Norway's contribution, and we agree to both examine this matter in committee and have an open discussion. After the operation, we got no UN force in, and the population stood alone after the brutal and authoritarian leader was killed. People experienced a humanitarian crisis and a lack of basic security. It led to a vacuum of power that was not filled by democratic forces, but by various warlords. The experience is that if we're going to go in, we have to have the capacity and the will to stabilize society afterwards. Some of the same experiences took place in the world's society in Iraq. We in Norway were against the intervention in 2003. The international forces entering Iraq probably left the country prematurely, giving ISIL free rein to establish its caliphate in 2014. Back to the Libya evaluation: Norwegian forces made a very good effort in Libya. The military objectives were achieved. This shows how capable people we have in the Norwegian defense. They deserve a thank you from us who decided to send them out to participate in an operation with a United Nations mandate. What was missing was a political solution following the fall of the Gaddafi regime. It was the Stortinget who asked for this evaluation, as we previously asked for the evaluation of the efforts in Afghanistan. In my view, it would be right to evaluate our participation in the fight against ISIL and the efforts for stabilisation in Iraq when the time comes. We know such reviews are important in order to gain experience. In the evaluation, one indicates that the decision was taken in line with constitutional practice. It can be argued that there should have been a meeting held earlier in the enlarged Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence. There was also a disagreement between the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs as to whether the participation of Norwegian forces was legitimate under international law. It should have been clear before we went to the debate in this room, which of the definitions was based on. I also think it is right to update the Basic Law so that the wording is more consistent with current practice. This is also in line with the recommendations of the sample. A decision in Stortinget at that time had not changed Norway’s position. At the time, there was consultation in the enlarged Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence, there was a formal decision in the Council of State, and there was a debate here in the Grand Chamber. And if instead there had been voting in Stortinget after the discussion, the decision would have been the same. All parties supported the action in Libya. Our belief that the Arab Spring should lead to democracy and free societies throughout the Middle East today appears to be an utopian dream. The whole region has taken more steps back, and the sprouts of democratic democracy have been shut down. A lot went wrong afterwards. However, the debate in Stortinget on the UN's clear mandate for the operation was relatively nuanced. The Prime Minister stressed that there were no ideal alternatives to action. As regards the view on the use of military power, the Prime Minister said in 2011: ‘The use of military power does not in itself lead to a political solution to the complex conflicts in Libya. Yes, a military intervention can in itself make such conflicts deeper. There must therefore be a very high threshold for the use of military power in international relations. In the case of Libya, the world society faced several far from perfect alternatives to action.’ With political views of being in principle against all military interventions, even when civil life is at stake, action options are lacking once a mandate has been given by the UN Security Council. Action paralysis during the genocides of Rwanda and Srebrenica is the background of the doctrine Responsible to protect. That the world community did not do enough to avert these genocides was a serious trigger, and it became clear that military intervention in given situations is necessary. Negative experience after the Libya operation took a long time before some intervened militarily against ISIL's cruel behaviour in Syria and Iraq. It may appear in the future to be a sin of omission. I do not claim that decisions on military intervention are simple. Sometimes it can also have unintended consequences. It can lead to increasing upsurges in extreme terrorist groups, and where an authoritarian regime falls, the vacuum of power can be filled by equal authoritarian leaders or competing terrorist groups. Both parts create fear and instability for the civilian population. But – and this is important – when you fear genocide and face a difficult ethical choice, it is not to let go of doing the same thing as not making any mistakes. No, as part of a world society, we have a responsibility to make our contribution to prevent human suffering where we can. That's why we went into Libya with a clear UN mandate. That is why we have taken part in the military to stop ISIL, and that is why we took part in Kosovo, even without a UN mandate, to prevent genocide. It was just such a situation we faced when the Prime Minister explained to Stortinget that we had extensive information about the development of an emergency situation for Libya's civilian population. He said, «The world witnessed an authoritarian ruler who met peaceful protests with sharp shots and heavy shooting. The situation evolved toward a civil war. Colonel Gaddafi clearly stated that his intention was to crush the opposition, and that there would be no mercy." That is why we took part, and I believe that the evaluation, presentation, comitology and an open debate in the main hall are important to gain experience from that type of military operation. ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e758	The review of the committee shows that the Norwegian decisions on military participation in the Libya operation were characterised by four unique aspects: Both the UN, NATO and our closest allies felt that military use of power was necessary. The Security Council had managed to reach an agreement on a mandate for the use of military power to protect civilians. There was support from regional actors, including the Arab League. A Norwegian contribution to military intervention had support in all parties in Stortinget. The combination of these conditions led to the decision on a Norwegian strength contribution. The strength contribution played a significant role in the first six months of the Libyan operation. There was broad international support for the intervention itself. We have many examples from history where the lack of intervention has had fatal consequences. Norwegian military efforts in Libya were assessed to be appropriate for central Norwegian interests. Through military efforts, Norway demonstrated the will and ability to implement UN decisions and to support NATO and central allies in NATO cooperation. In addition, through military efforts, Norway could also demonstrate support for global initiatives to protect civilians. The unique foreign policy situation that existed when the Security Council approved the use of military power to protect civilians in Libya made it difficult for the government to stand on the sideline in assessing whether Norway should contribute to these operations. Despite scepticism for a military contribution in parts of the government, there were few counter-conceptions about Norway’s participation. A strong media press also contributed to it. The risk of not acting was considered to be greater than the risk of not acting. The Norwegian authorities faced several challenges, in particular the understanding of developments in Libya. Norway had very limited knowledge of Libya when the uprising started in February 2011. In such situations, decision-makers often have to rely on information from the media and other countries. It is a strength of the Norwegian political system that it is possible for the government to take decisions at short notice in the field of foreign and security policy. This provides for the freedom, efficiency and flexibility necessary in international crisis management. That is why it is a little perceptive, the comment of the Central Party in dealing with this matter, a note of the Central Party stands alone, whereby the consultation obligation of the Government towards the Stortinget today is to be extended to a full plenary session in Stortinget, whether Norway should participate in military operations, and in its ultimate consequence, go to war. I am asking questions as to whether that comment is carefully considered as to its intended and unintended consequences. It is important that the mechanisms for sharing information between government and growth be further developed in order to facilitate a common understanding of the situation and speed when the situation so requires. Consultation with the organs of the Great Britain should take place as soon as possible and should be well anchored in the government. In recent years, Norwegian participation in international operations has often been met with objections related to the understanding of the Basic Law §§ 25 and 26. The wording of the Basic Law opens up for interpretation, and current constitutional practices may create unnecessary controversy related to the constitutionality of participation in international operations. It is not to be overlooked that the Committee expresses a sympathy, both between the lines and on the lines, as regards the recommendation of the sample to bring the wording of the Basic Law §25 and 26 more into line with current law.

They are really difficult, and there is no fasit that suits all countries and all situations. In the case of exactly Saudi Arabia, there is no doubt that we are talking about a regime that completely lacks respect for fundamental human rights. The Khashoggi case, on which the rapporteur was involved, has rightly received a lot of attention, but it is by no means a unique case. There's no room in the few minutes I have at my disposal here, to go into the seriousness of the situation in Saudi Arabia, but torture, death penalty, prisoners of conscience, extensive female repression are punctuated. It happens with a backstop where Saudi Arabia tries to increase its regional power and take regional leadership. This is why it is important – and it is right – that Norway reacts sharply and gives clear notice both directly to the Saudi authorities and in multilateral forums about what we think about developments in Saudi Arabia. In the view of the Labour Party, it was also quite right of Norway, after the very alarming reports we received from the major humanitarian actors present in Yemen, to put an end to new licences for the export of B-materials or multi-use goods for military use to Saudi Arabia. I also agree here with the rapporteur's expectations for the review of the legislation to come. In terms of SPU, we have developed and strengthened the ethical guidelines for many years. The important thing about those guidelines is that they are the basis of all our investments – all investments in all companies in all countries. It's not that many years since we put in and reinforced the work on ethics in the SPU, so we're giving general guidelines on human rights to be followed up by the fund as a whole. The size does not go in and points to individual countries or to individual cases. It may, of course, be very difficult in some cases, but I think it's important. It is the line that has made it possible for the fund to withdraw — to take an example — from companies that have contributed to the building of the wall of international law on the West Bank, without being able to be interpreted as or seen as a political incited response to a single country. I'm concerned that the legitimacy and the impact of ethics work is weakened if we rock that it happens on criteria that are the same for all companies. That being said, SPU is moving into increasingly difficult parts of the world, and the Financial Committee itself has pointed out the importance of the fund entering a country where there is reason to be concerned about the human rights situation, that we have rules that are good enough, and not least that we have enough resources in the Ethics Council to make sure that we are not inside companies that we shouldn't be inside. I am not sure that we are today, and it should be a debate when the SPU message comes in just a short time. Finally, I would like to refer to the Workers' Party's special remark about the sanctions regime against people for gross violations of human rights. The setting of the Granavolden platform also indicates that this should be done within a common European solution. It's important, of course, that you have a lot of countries with you, but if you're going to sit and wait for everyone to join you, it can take a long time to get completely toothless. So we propose that we look at the possibility of a smaller group of related countries joining forces on such a mechanism. With that, I take up the Labour Party's proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e1147	It may sometimes be necessary, especially after hearing the representative Christian Tybring-Gjedde, to remind someone in the main hall of what human rights are actually for something. Human rights are about a minimum standard of how the world's country has defined human treatment. It's absolutely basic, it's normative, it's going on across different cultures -- it's a minimum standard. Nothing shall be accepted if it violates the minimum standard. In addition, human rights are enshrined in international agreements concluded between countries. The wonderful thing about these agreements is that they give both the right and the obligation to criticize each other. That is why sensible monitoring mechanisms and reporting mechanisms have been put in place to keep each other in mind in different countries. This is largely embedded in a multilateral system, in which Norway is a very active contributor – and that is very, very fine. In addition, it may sometimes be necessary for a country to directly criticise another country, a type of bilateral criticism. The Committee also points out that these themes, including the country's defence and justice sectors, as well as its preparedness and social security, have been addressed in a variety of investigations and policy processes over the past few years. There is reason to take a positive view of the increased public and political attention to issues related to country security and preparedness, given a worsening threat picture as a backtrace. Political debate and increased public attention to the country's defences, police, preparedness and social security have also given increased public awareness of the field, as well as higher political priorities of society's ability to protect. The Committee stresses that this is a good that cannot be taken for granted and that these are areas that must be worked on every day. It will be up to the head of defence and the defence to give priority to others, so that the strengths on the basis of operational assessments provide overall the best response in the current threat scenario, and thus also the best and strongest defence of the country as a whole. The above-mentioned majority also emphasises that the Home Protection system should remain nationwide, but that the security policy situation calls for increased involvement in the Home Protection system in North Norway. There is also reason to recall that the government has a clear commitment to the Heimevater in the north, including that units in the north should be equipped with heavier weapons, such as light air protection and heavier pan-server weapons. Police personnel have increased significantly, with nearly 2,800 more employees since New Year 2013, of which about 1,650 are police trained. It underlines that more than 1,400 police posts have been assigned to the police districts. There is also a large-scale communication on police capacity and competence needs. It is necessary to assess whether the best way to make use of the overall economic and human resources of the police is to make the target of two police officers per thousand inhabitants per police district. The crime scene has changed dramatically in a few years, and the police reform therefore reorganises the police so that it is better adapted to today’s crime scene. Traditional mining crime has partly moved from the streets to the digital spaces, across national borders, and it is therefore necessary to streamline police resources to areas where police need to deal with these kinds of challenges. The majority of the Committee suggests that some police districts will necessarily have more staff than others, relative to the population and the extent of crime.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e1376	This government has made a significant effort on the Norwegian police force. In line with the government’s objective of achieving a police coverage of two police officers per thousand inhabitants at national level during 2020, funds have been allocated this year to recruit newly trained police students. Police staff have increased by about 2 800 yearly works from the end of 2013 until February 2019. Of these, there are 1,650 police posts, about 1,450 of which have been assigned to the police districts. Police coverage has now increased from 1.71 in the fourth quarter of 2013 to 1.95 in the fourth quarter of 2018. So we're in the right direction to achieve the goal of two per thousand. The draft representative asks the government to set the target of two officials per thousand inhabitants at district level. As stated in the opinions common to the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of Justice on this Document 8, we believe that setting a staffing rate of two per thousand at district level is not appropriate. The reason for this is that crime rates vary greatly between different districts. It is important that we have districts that have a crew that are adapted to the challenges facing individual districts. This also has a cost effect. There are some districts that have a significant overpressure in terms of two per thousand, but if all the others are to be raised at that level, it has an annual cost of 2.4 billion. kr. Then you have to ask yourself, can this money be used in a better way? I therefore believe it is a little wise approach to change the objective of applying to the district level. We should go away from managing resource efforts, actions and activities, and rather facilitate police and police officers to make use of resources as efficiently as possible. We must control priorities and objectives. It's a more strategic and long-term police management. Furthermore, the representatives ask the government to ensure that funds are provided to support the work and to ensure better basic protection of protective objects. The State budget for 2019 made it clear that the Police Directorate has put in place a comprehensive plan of permanent and temporary measures for object protection, the aim of which is to close the deviations identified by the National Audit Office. The five-year plan, which also includes the securing of operational centres not classified as shielding objects, is cost-implemented to 300 million by the Directorate of Police. kr. The plan prioritises the displayable object and the Police Directorate plans that the residual risk of the current shielding object will be within the provisions of the new Security Act, as assessed by the Police Directorate, during 2020. Following the government's proposal, Stortinget decided to increase the allocation to the police for object protection work by 171 million. SEK in 2019, where 142 million. NOK of them is linked to the plan that I have just mentioned. Twenty-nine million. kr goes to redundancy measures, in addition to funds related to data centres, PST and a new emergency centre. The agencies have a good dialogue, regular meetings and carry out several exercises together where the focus is on getting better cooperation at all levels. I want to bring forward the annual exercise Nordlys that helps to increase cooperation and counter-terrorism efforts for both sectors. Finally, I would like to say that with regard to the transport readiness for the police in North Norway, I refer to the current system where the Defence Bell 412 helicopters at Bardufoss, which have an hour's response time for police use, are to be maintained until another system is in place, and we are working on it.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e1867	As the small country we are after all, Norway is totally dependent on the respect and defence of the international legal order. Our own security presupposes that the world community speaks loudly when the principles are broken. That's why we have to be coherent and expressive in all contexts internationally when international law rules are challenged. It should not be our own short-term interests that lie at the root of our opinion on violations of international law or human rights. All UN Member States must respect the inviolability of borders, the Charter of the United Nations and the right of peoples to self-determination, and all UN Member States are obliged to call out when borders and rights are put to the test. Morocco had no right to illegally invade the non-autonomous Western Sahara territory. The territory was inhabited by a people. This people had – and still have – a right to decide for themselves the future of their territory, and a legitimate right to choose independence from colonial times. The occupation was condemned by the UN, and the General Assembly asked Morocco immediately to withdraw its troops. They didn't. That is precisely why it is so positive that the position of changing Norwegian governments is fixed. Norway does not recognise Morocco’s annexation and occupation of the territory. In particular, our position was clear when Norway was in the Security Council, and in the years that followed. Under Per-Kristian Foss as Minister of Finance, for example: the following regarding the Kerr-McGee oil exploration company in the territory: ‘Morocco has occupied Western Sahara for a number of years, despite strong condemnation from the United Nations. Following the Council’s assessment, Kerr-McGee facilitates the possible use of the natural resources in the region by Morocco. The Council considers such activities to be a ‘in particular a serious breach of fundamental ethical standards’, inter alia, because this may help to legitimise Morocco’s sovereignty requirements and thereby undermine the UN peace process.» Similarly clear statements we have had many of them, both before and after. It is important, and I will commend it, because the humanitarian consequences of the continued occupation are serious. Firstly, half of the population of Western Sahara has been displaced. In recent years, the situation has become increasingly critical by international donors withdrawing and leaving the refugees to themselves. The UN adjusted last year the population figures of the refugee camps, while the UN Secretary-General calls for increased support. It is essential that Norway step up humanitarian aid for this population, and in particular the nutritional situation is critical. Over the last two years, the leadership of the Norwegian youth parties has seen the importance of long-term Norwegian food aid. Anemia is widespread, while maternal mortality is still very high. People live on business in emergency aid in the fourth decade, and unfortunately U.S. stopped the long-term food aid for the weakest refugees in 2018. Patients are now no longer offered nutrition. Secondly, while the situation is very critical in the refugee camps, as we know, there is also a serious attack on the Saharan who lives in that part of Western Sahara that is under illegal Moroccan occupation. The UN Special Rapporteur on Arbitration and Torture has reported on torture against Saharan activists, and the UN peacekeeping operation in Western Sahara, MINURSO, has been prevented from reporting on the human rights situation in the territory. It's the only modern UN operation that doesn't have such an opportunity. In the latest ranking of the Freedom House organization, only seven territories and countries around the world score worse than Western Sahara on political freedoms. Morocco’s closest ally, France, is using its place in the UN Security Council to block the possibility of the UN operation reporting on the abuses. Therefore, it is very positive that the Norwegian Government has repeatedly raised the issue of respect for human rights with French political leadership. I want to commend the government for being clear on that point. It is very important that work be continued vis-à-vis Paris, and preferably in cooperation with the other Nordic countries. In addition to stopping the reporting of human rights and their violation, France is also blocking the UN processes. Because of the French authorities, the United Nations Security Council has been virtually paralysed on the issue ever since the invasion took place. It was actually going to be an impatient Trump administration to kick the conversations off again after many years of standstill. The fact that Morocco has finally accepted the way back to the negotiating table is good, but as long as Morocco does not face further political pressure, it is unlikely that they will at this time be willing to deny the requirement of permanent and illegal annexation of the territory. It is therefore frustrating to see the EU ignoring judges in the EU Court of Justice on Western Sahara under French influence. While Norwegian governments have been aware that Norway’s agreements with Morocco obviously cannot apply to Western Sahara, the EU is of a different opinion. The European Court of Justice has concluded that EU-Morocco agreements cannot be covered by Western Sahara, but these judgments have in practice chosen by the EU institutions to put in a drawer. I asked last November what the government would do to deal with our closest European allies that the EU cannot conclude trade agreements covering Western Sahara. The Minister of Foreign Affairs replied at the time that the EU was well-known in its Norwegian position, but in the same reply the government pointed out that it is Norwegian that the ‘interests of the local population’ must be respected. It is important that that particular aspect is really considered irrelevant by previous EU judgments. What is relevant in the Norwegian policy of renunciation and, as the government often mentions in the Western Sahara context, is the aspect that such business must be in line with the wishes of the Saharan people. This right of consent for the people of Western Sahara has been mentioned consistently, through alternating governments, in the context of the important dissuade against business in the territory. The right to consent was extended, inter alia, to: drawn up by Minister of Foreign Affairs Brende when we last had an interpellation debate on this topic – in 2014, by representative Trine Skei Grande. The aspect of consent is worth mentioning often — and more often — as it places Norwegian policy perfectly in line with what the European Court of Justice also concludes. It is not Norway's job or the company's job to find out how business life in Western Sahara is for the benefit of those who live there. They have to figure it out on their own. In this context, there is a difference between the words ‘local population’ and ‘people’. Those who are going to tell us how they want their lives and business organized are the people of Western Sahara themselves, and not the Moroccan people who have moved in. The then Secretary of State Eide stated in 2011 that it was obvious that the Saharan people and not the immigrants were meant when talking about the important policy of dissuadement. State Secretary Helgesen provided similar clarifications in the NRC back in 2005, and it is important that this self-evidentness be repeated. So, from the Norwegian side, let us continue to use the term ‘people’ when we talk about Western Sahara’s people’s rights, as the Norwegian government often does, and is also in line with the European Court of Justice. Finally, few international violations of international law are as obvious as those we see in Western Sahara. The games at Stortinget are in agreement. The Great Lakes Friends Group for Western Sahara is composed of representatives from all parties. Very constructive questions have been asked from all parties in recent years, and therefore we hope that a clear defence for international law will continue to be Norwegian policy in the years to come.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e1902	First, I want to thank the interpellant for traveling the interpellant. The conflict in the Western Sahara has been going on for more than 40 years, with great human costs and humanitarian consequences. According to the United Nations, there are more than 170 000 Saharan refugees living in camps in Tindouf, Algeria, and conditions in the camps are difficult. The humanitarian situation in the refugee camps is particularly difficult, and Norway supports a project under the auspices of the UN High Commissioner for Refugees in the Camps. So it's right, as the representative pointed out, that the support that had been in the camps for nearly 17 years was stopped from last year. It was an assessment of the achievement of results and quality that did so. However, this does not prevent the future consideration of other humanitarian projects that can contribute. Too many young people live in camps with no prospect of a better future, and a political solution to the conflict will be important both for the people affected by it and for regional security and stability. It is a positive sign that the UN Secretary-General's Special Mission for Western Sahara, Horst Köhler, has held two rounds of talks since December last year and that all parties and neighbouring countries, including Morocco, Algeria, Mauritania and Polisario, have participated. According to the participants, the dynamics of the meetings have been positive. In the Joint Final Declaration of the last March meeting, the parties stated that they would continue the discussions. The path to a lasting political solution will be long, and a lot of work remains. At the same time, it is important, in our view, that dialogue continue and that the parties continue to build mutual trust, especially when difficult issues are discussed. Norway's position in principle is that all UN operations should have a human rights mandate. That is why we believe that the mandate for UN peacekeeping operations in the Western Sahara should also include monitoring and reporting of the human rights situation in the area covered by the UN operation. But this is still not supported by the UN Security Council. At the same time, it is positive that the Security Council encourages the parties to increase cooperation with the UN High Commissioner for Human Rights, including visits by the High Commissioner of the region. We believe that such a visit will be useful for all parties. From the Norwegian side, the Rabat embassy has regular contact with the Moroccan Human Rights Commission, including their offices in Western Sahara. We address the human rights situation in Western Sahara and the camps in Tindouf with both Moroccan and Algerian authorities, as well as with representatives of Polisario. In recent years, the European Court of Justice has taken three decisions that EU bilateral agreements with Morocco cannot be applied in Western Sahara. The Court of Justice understands that the territory cannot be considered as part of its territory. Despite the judgment, the European Parliament supported the new agricultural agreement between the EU and Morocco in January this year. This includes goods from Western Sahara. Similarly, in February the European Parliament decided to support the new fisheries agreement, which includes the sea areas outside Western Sahara. It can be expected that, like the previous ones, the proceedings may be challenged in the judicial system. From the Norwegian side, we have been particularly concerned with avoiding actions that could be seen as legitimizing the situation in Western Sahara. An example of this is our interpretation and practice of the free trade agreement between EFTA and Morocco. This Agreement indicates that it applies to the territories of the Contracting Parties. From the Norwegian side it is clear that, in our view, the agreement does not cover products originating in Western Sahara. The other EFTA States interpret the Agreement in the same way. Based on advice from the Ethics Council, the State pension fund abroad has also excluded investments in several companies engaged in oil-related activities off the coast of Western Sahara on behalf of Morocco. The code of international law imposes a number of obligations and restrictions on Morocco towards the people of the Western Sahara as regards Moroccan exploitation of natural resources in and in the off-Saharan sea areas. Such activities must be carried out in accordance with the wishes of the population concerned and in a way that benefits the population. If Morocco facilitates trade, investment, resource use and other commercial activities in Western Sahara which are not in accordance with the wishes of the local population, this could be contrary to international law. In the current situation in Western Sahara, it will in most cases be very difficult for Norwegian enterprises to predict the impact of concrete investments and industrial activities and whether they will benefit the local population. In order to avoid these kinds of situations, the then government decided in 2007 to adopt a precautionary approach and, in general, to be cautious about investing in this area. This was done in the form of an official defamation. The Norwegian advice does not constitute a legally binding prohibition, but is intended to encourage private actors to exercise prudence and social responsibility when assessing their involvement in the area. Norwegian positions in the case of Western Sahara are firm and in line with the decisions of the United Nations Security Council. Norway supports the UN's efforts to contribute to a political solution to the conflict, which, according to the Security Council, must imply the right of self-determination for the people of Western Sahara. It is therefore positive that the parties now wish to continue the dialogue under the leadership of the UN Special Mission. On the Norwegian side, we will continue to contact all parties to the conflict and visit Western Sahara and the camps in Tindouf.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e1969	For a whole year, the then 21 - year - old Saharan woman Nazha El Khalidi was in Norway. Here she went on the photo line at a high school in South Africa. On December 4, 2018, she was arrested in Western Sahara for having the livestream demonstration on Facebook. She belongs to a group of journalists called Equipe Media. Sahrawi journalists operate in secret. They do not have a press card or union protection because Morocco has criminalised all forms of independent journalism relating to Western Sahara by means of Articles 265 to 7 of the Moroccan Criminal Code. Like all other Sahrawi journalists, Nazha is therefore working in violation of all Moroccan legislation and without protection. On 4 March, according to Article 381 of the Moroccan Criminal Code, Nazha was accused of calling himself a journalist without a press card. She is now in danger of being sentenced to two years in prison for calling herself a journalist, and she is going to trial in a few weeks. ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e2097	Initially, as the rapporteur, I would like to start by thanking the Committee for a good cooperation in this matter of equal pay, which has come from various representatives of the Socialist Left Party. The Committee has set itself behind the intentions of the proposers to balance pay differentials in Norwegian working life. What this issue has been about are various proposals on how to balance pay differentials between sexes and counteract the gender-divided labour market. The Committee held an open hearing on this draft representative on 31 January this year, and I would also like to thank all those who contributed to that hearing. At the beginning of the debate, it is also important to mention that today we have regulated discrimination in terms of wages, quite comprehensive in our legislation. The principle of equal pay for equal work is laid down by law, and the right to demand information about the salaries of others if they are suspected of being discriminated against in respect of remuneration is also statutory. In addition, I would like to point out that the social partners have a central role to play in the work on equal pay, both in the implementation of wage and salary settlements themselves, and not least in cooperation with the authorities when the work on equal pay is followed up in relevant selections and advice. What this debate is therefore essentially about is how to counteract structural wage differences despite existing regulation and legislation. For the left, we have also been very concerned about helping to reduce gender pay differentials in Norwegian working life. In particular, I think it is interesting to bring forward the figures from the SSB that show that a major reason for persistent pay differentials between women and men is that women often choose low paid professions, while men choose professions with higher wages. I think one of the terms that came up at our hearing is particularly interesting, because it gives a slightly different look at this particular question. It's not necessarily the case that only women choose work at lower wages, it's also something structural in our society that says something about how we value different types of work. Then it is simply that traditional women's churches are systematically less paid than traditional men choose. But one of the proposals that comes up here today is just about doing something about the pay gap within the same industry, not about the differences that exist between different types of work. It's about this model of Iceland, to which there's a lot of evidence. I have been so lucky that as part of the delegation to the Nordic Council, I was able to participate and look at how this equal pay standard and this certification system work. What we know is that we know pretty little, because the work on this island model hasn't gotten that far, and at least we haven't got the results of it yet. So I think it's too early to go into a debate about importing that system into Norway. Finally, I would like to say that I am pleased that the government has several different programmes that are just going to make sure that it does something about these structural pay differentials, programmes such as ‘Human in Health’ and ‘Women and Technology’, which encourage young people to choose untraditionally – but not least for the other concrete decisions we have taken in this committee in the past, among other things. in the case of the obligation of activity and presentation which will be important for making enterprises aware of the existing wage differences in their own industry.

That is why we also have a sex-shared labour market, and that is why very many women work part-time. It is therefore not consistent with a single measure. There's no such thing as a quick fix. There are today discussions within the Employment and Pension Policy Council, but not their own cooperation. ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e2146	The right behind is the intention of the proposers to balance gender pay differentials in Norwegian working life. At the same time, we look at numbers from the SSB that male specialist nurses earned NOK 39 960 more than female colleagues. Professor Bente Abrahamsen of the Center for Professional Studies at OsloMet believes different preferences can be one of the reasons for the pay gap between male and female nurses. She says that differences in pay may be due to different income and working time priorities. Men give higher priority to pay, while women often give priority to parttime or organised work. We see from the research that men and women make different choices based on the different preferences they have, which in turn affect income. In the nursing profession, where income is largely determined by how much you work, and which shifts you take, the differences may also be related to this. Consumer economist at DNB Silje Sandmæl came in March this year with an invitation to take the gender equality in business one step further. She said that women now have to take the paycheck, even if it is unpleasant. She encourages women to talk more about pay at work: ‘In many jobs it is taboo to talk about wages. Who has the most to lose on it?" Those who are unfairly paid lose the fact that they are not talking about pay. Sandmæl refers to a survey of wages and negotiations recently carried out by the bank in the population. There is evidence that more women than men feel unfairly paid. 41 pst. By the men they say they get the salary they deserve, and 56 pst. says they should earn more, while only 29 pst. By the women they say they get the pay they deserve, and 69 pst. says they should have earned more. The survey also shows that most people find it more unpleasant than satisfactory to negotiate pay with their employer. But it pays off, it shows us the numbers. The whole of seven out of ten people who have negotiated pay have achieved better conditions. Of the women who have negotiated, 65 have pst. received a better salary, against only 46 pst. of the men. Sandsmæl explains this trend that women are more likely to win in wage negotiations, because women often do not ask for higher wages until they are 110 pst themselves. ensure that they deserve precisely the higher pay. This trend is also seen in female jobseekers, who would like to meet all the qualifications of a post before applying, while men only meet the majority of the qualifications before applying for the job concerned. The government has a broad and ongoing work to prevent gender-divided educational and professional choices. The fact that both sexes are represented throughout the range of professions is important for creating good role models, ensuring good recruitment, gaining different perspectives and representation — and not least reducing pay differentials.

This just underlines that we need a more obligating plan. It would be wise for the government to take the initiative in what is proposed in proposals No. 2 and 3 of the proposal for a representative, which seeks to ask the social partners to present concrete ways of initiating cooperation between the parties in which the same parties are to present concrete solutions for the balancing of wage differences, measures to combat the sex-shared labour market, etc. The Left Party believes that there is a need for special efforts to reduce the large pay gap between men and women. One can read on their own websites that they ‘will ask the social partners to help correct the gender pay gap by designing a timetable for when this is to happen’. This is similar to the SV proposed today. I am therefore surprised that the Minister in his letter to the Committee responds that she sees no need to initiate cooperation between the parties on equal pay issues. What's happened here? I have to admit that I had probably expected the Left to have more impact on its equality policy in this area – when they sit with the Minister for Gender Equality.
