ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e2231	In 1975, the UN Women's Year, women in Iceland went from their jobs around two o'clock in the afternoon. It was a starting point for the equality uprising we're still fighting 44 years later, because 44 years later, women still earn only $87 on average for every hundred bill a man earns. Last year, on the same date as in 1975, SV marked the equal pay uprising by promoting the proposal we are discussing today. The action in this country stems from a decision at the Trondheim Conference and is targeted by trade union workers and activists throughout the country. What motivates the action and the struggle is an important fact. It's that without economic autonomy we'll never achieve real equality between women and men. For example, this is a case in point. on all the small everyday decisions, those taken around the kitchen table, who has the last word when the family is going to buy a new car, who has the last word when the family might want to establish itself and take up mortgages. I believe that around many kitchen tables in Norway, the person with the highest income also has the greatest power in large and small decisions. That power means something in a everyday life, and it can't be understated. But even though we and the generations before us have been fighting for equal pay for decades, we're still far away from goals. As mentioned above, women still earn $87 on average for every hundred bills a man earns. But even worse, depending on how you calculate it, it will take between 50 and 100 years to achieve equal pay at today's rate. It clearly shows that we do not have a policy of equality and equal pay as monks, with today's government. The last 40 years of gender equality have shown that active policies are needed to correct the unfair structures that give women less power and opportunities than men. It is also necessary to combat the discrimination against value added in Norwegian working life, where women’s dominated professions are paid less than men’s dominated professions. And it is necessary to combat the most recent elements of wage discrimination in each activity and sector, where, for example, the Equality and Discrimination Ombudsman. is clear that there is still a lack of equal pay. Therefore, as part of the gender equality action, SV has proposed the measures we are dealing with today. First of all, we look at Iceland, where legislation on equal pay has been strengthened far more than what we have here in Norway, through, among other things. moving the burden of proof to the employer, so it is the employer who has to prove that equal pay is paid for work of equal value. In addition, this shall be checked by an external party. We want the same thing in Norway. It is a strengthening of the legislation we already have. Then it is also essential for us in SV that the social partners participate in the design of such a scheme here. Secondly, we want the state to take the initiative in co-operation between the parties for equal pay. Both measures to combat the gender-based labour market, which we so often talk about, measures to increase full-time culture, which are certainly an important part of this, and also a possible plan for an equal pay pot. We do not escape an escalating, multi-annual equal pay pot in order to balance the discrimination in the value of work. It emerged in negotiations between the parties – that is where it needs to be – but the state should at least say that we are using the funds, if we find a mechanism. Thirdly, this proposal for a representative is part of a larger package for equal pay and equality in employment because it is about the whole, it is about equal sharing of care in the home, so we have proposed improvements in the leave system, and it is not least about the part-time trap we still have in Norway, and therefore we have also proposed the right to full time in another part of our work. Several decades of fighting for equal pay are not yet in goal. We don't have time to wait for the pace of today's government. Therefore, active policies are needed to balance the differences in power, wealth and opportunities between women and men.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e2254	Equal pay for equal work is an important and fundamental principle of equality, and there is a broad political consensus that we must also work here in Norway to close the pay gap between men and women. The two main reasons for the uneven pay distribution between men and women are that we still have a widely shared labour market here to a large extent, and that far more women than men work part-time. Here I would like to stress that the Christian People's Party clearly distinguishes between voluntary and involuntary part-time work. That parents in the busy early childhood choose to work part-time if they have a chance to do so is a good thing that we politicians should hang out and cheer up. We also need to bring with us the fact that someone actually wants to work part-time, for example to free up time to care for old parents or to volunteer in different ways, and the alternative value creation that it provides must not be underestimated. That being said, we know that there are too many women who want to work full time, who don't get the opportunity, and it has to be taken care of. In particular in the public sector, there has been a systematic effort to address the challenges of involuntary part-time work and equal pay problems, and efforts have been identified that seem to help over time. I still think it's worth getting inspired by the grips Iceland has taken by introducing – as the world's first country – an equal pay bill. This law requires all enterprises with more than 25 employees to report on the equal pay situation, and where men earn more than women for the same work done, fines are imposed. But also here in the country, we have the principle of equal pay. Section 34 of the Gender Equality Act states that «women and men in the same activity shall have equal pay for the same work or work of equal value». The Christian People's Party is going to follow the effect of the relatively recently introduced equal pay law in Iceland and continuously assess the learning outcomes and the transfer value we can take from there. I would also like to point out that the government is proposing a strengthening of the obligation of activity and reporting, and that is exactly what will be a good tool, because the monitoring of gender-based pay differentials will make the differences visible where they exist, thus facilitating the implementation of targeted measures.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e2269	Norway is among the world's most equal countries, but I agree with both the proposers and everyone who has spoken here earlier today, that we still have a way to go. But the differences between women and men are getting smaller and smaller. It applies in employment, in working hours, in management figures and in parental pension schemes, as well as in wages and salaries. Differences between women's and men's average wages continue to decrease, but still women make 87.1 pst. of a man's salary when we include both full-time and part-time employees. An important explanation of this pay gap is the gender-based labour market we still have in Norway. Women and men have different types of jobs, in different industries, in different sectors, and they are exposed to different types of wage formation. But sail if you sneak in and look at women who work in the same profession, the same industry, the same sector, have the same education and are as old as men, it's still that women make 93.5 pst. of what is the wages of the man. There is no law on pay discrimination in Norway, and we are now working on a proposal to strengthen the obligation of activity and reporting for employers in public and private enterprises. The government is going to come back to it soon. Surveying of earnings provides a basis for assessing the existence of pay differentials between women and men in comparable positions. The employer can assess whether there is a risk of gender discrimination and whether preventive action is required. Good guidance for the employer is important. Therefore, the Equality and Discrimination Ombudsman will help all to pursue equality and follow up the obligation of activity and presentation. The gender-based labour market is largely due to gender-based educational choices. In the Granavolden platform, we therefore say that we should stimulate more people to choose non-traditional education and careers. And there are signs of change in the sex-shared labour market. Women are increasingly involved in education and occupations that have been dominated by men, such as medicine, law, psychology and economics. The problem is that there has not been a similar change among men. A particular challenge is getting men into women-dominated professions. The Government therefore proposes to present a separate strategy that will contribute to a more equal education and labour market. The strategy shall be based on the work of the UNGIDag Committee and shall include, inter alia: propose good measures to encourage young people to break gender-based education choices. We have increased support for girls and technology, and we aim to get more girls into technology and real science. What really wants to promote gender mainstreaming in working life is to get more men into the health and social sector and into other women's dominated professions. We have therefore used funds to get more men to apply for primary school and kindergarten teacher training, as the Minister of Higher Education and Research has also presented today. We also want more men in health education and care. There is a great need in the labour market for people with these training courses. We have also taken initiatives in tripartite cooperation to involve them in action on recruitment to the different professions, and in other actions we can take in tripartite cooperation to increase equality between women and men's wages. We cooperate, both with documentation and with research, on action with all social partners. Another important step taken in this field is the third division of parental leave. It will also lead to us balancing pay differentials over time, because we are going to see that the argument used against many women in their wage development can no longer be used against them, because we want to see a more equal withdrawal of parental leave between women and men. I'm pretty sure we'll also see that it will affect wage developments and pay differentials between women and men. So we're going to come up with more grips within reporting obligations and requirements. We're going to come up with more measures to reduce the gender divide of the labour market that we have, and we're going to cooperate with the social partners to combat the inequalities we see today.

In addition, in this area, the central party believes that local authorities must have the freedom to organise the supply according to local needs and demand. However, it is important that equipment borrowed is of good quality and safe to use. That is why we have promoted our own proposal, which has a different wording from that of SVS, and which preserves local freedom. The government has notified a child and youth report. We in the Central Party expect the issues raised in the draft representative that we are debating here today to be further raised in that communication, with municipal autonomy as an important part. I look forward to that. With that, I take up the proposal of the Centre Party in the matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e2698	I would like to thank the Cooperation Committee as the rapporteur for the draft representative of the Landfill Representatives Freddy André Øvstegård, Karin Andersen and Audun Lysbakken on the Children's Protection Lift II. The committee had written consultations on the proposal, with a deadline of 31 January, and as far as I have registered, no written submissions to the committee have been received within the time limit. It has not been said that the theme does not take up or engage, because the representatives of the SV address important issues and promote three concrete proposals, which aim to increase the staffing of municipal child protection – a four-year plan of escalation with earmarked funds, to assess an indicative recruitment standard in which case handlers should have no more than 15 cases, and to establish a lower recruitment limit in municipal child protection. Our Committee agrees with the proposers that children and young people who are at risk of care failure are one of the most vulnerable groups in society, and it is our job to ensure that they receive good help at the right time. Furthermore, the Committee considers that the quality of child protection should not be localised. Variations in the quality of municipal child protection services are unfortunate. All children shall have an equivalent offer. On behalf of the Committee, I would also like to stress that children and young people who are under the care of child protection must have a good support system around them, with professionally qualified staff who care for their rights, interests and needs. We in the Committee all agree that these children must be provided with a good aid. On the other hand, we do not always agree on the instruments. The respective parties may make their views known in their submissions. Afterwards, I will go into the opinions and suggestions of the Centre Party. We in the Central Party do not disagree with the intention of the proposal and share the concern. We see, in line with the proposers, the need to increase the staffing of municipal child protection. In some places, there is a high level of penetration, a high level of sick leave, work pressure and a long period of time. These are factors affecting both the quality and the ability of employees to provide good and sound services. The Joint Organisation and the Children’s Ombudsman have pointed out on several occasions that child protection needs to be provided with more staff. It is therefore strange, almost incomprehensible, that the government does not take these signals seriously. The centre party believes that a stepping-up plan is necessary to increase staffing, but we disagree that earmarked funds are the solution, as designation often leads to more bureaucracy. We have therefore put forward our own proposal asking the government to draw up a stepping-up plan with the aim of increasing staffing. In addition, we are promoting proposals such as: is designed to change the model of child protection management, where Bufeta's regional tasks are transferred to the county municipalities, where Bufdir is laid down and the Ministry of Children and Gender Equality is given the overall responsibility of legislator, budgetary officer and organiser of control and supervisory tasks, and where the role of the State as control and supervisory authority is strengthened. It is a little remarkable that the government that launched the regional reform, with a child and family minister from the Christian People's Party, a party that is truly committed to regional reform, does not follow the recommendations of its own expert committee on the tasks to be decentralised and transferred to the regions and municipalities. The committee proposed, among other things: – as the Centre Party – to deposit Bufetat and transfer its tasks to the counties. The government does not follow the recommendation of the expert committee, but instead chooses to re-examine whether the transfer of Bufatat's tasks in the protection of children to the county council will provide a reinforced offer to vulnerable children and families. Today, every time an employee ends up with an employer with a contribution pension scheme, a pension capital certificate is issued for the capital earned by the employee through the employment relationship, provided that the employment has lasted for more than 12 months. The cost of managing the pension capital must be borne by the employee himself. With the changes we are making now, it will be possible to collect pension capital certificates from previous employment and former individual pension savings on one pension scheme in the employer’s pension scheme, unless the employee reserves it. The purpose of this is to contribute to the more efficient management of pension funds under the pension scheme, in order to maximise pension provision for what has been earned and to make it easier to better understand your own earnings. It will also be possible for employees to choose to move both their past and current service to a supplier other than the one chosen by the current employer. We think it makes sense for the government and the Ministry of Finance to go a few rounds to find out how the cost allocation between the employee and the employer related to such a scheme can be optimally resolved. The important thing here is that you take into account that the goal is the most possible pension for each of the saved heads. Many claim – rightly – that pensions are complicated and inconclusive. Hopefully, this grip will help to make it a little less inconspicuous. However, some questions arise when major changes are made to a relatively complex system. Who shall bear the administrative costs related to the previous retirement pension? Is passive or negative consent appropriate? Who shall be responsible for providing the correct information? I believe that all these questions have either been answered well, or have been notified that you will return to them in the order. The most important thing to have in mind is in any case — and a advice to the government council on the way forward in the matter: how can individual employees get a higher pension for minimum bureaucracy? ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e3325	It is on behalf of a unanimous committee I present this vote. I would like to thank the Committee for both great goodwill and good cooperation in order to finalise the matter quickly. In the proposal currently under consideration, the Ministry of Local Government and Modernisation proposes to increase the grant of housing aid in 2019 to cover an exceptional payment to those who are beneficiaries of housing aid. This additional appropriation shall help to cover winter high electricity bills in low-income households. We have a winter behind us where we experienced in January and February that the power price was significantly higher than the average in the last five years. There are several reasons for this: There have been higher prices of CO2, gas and coal in Europe. In the summer of 2018 we experienced an extreme drought here at home. Warehouse fillings have been lower than normal. There's been little snow in the mountains. As everyone knows, our power consumption is highest in winter. Therefore, it was not until the winter months of 2018 and 2019 that ordinary households were fully aware of the consequences of the power situation. According to the SSB, the electricity prices for households were 41 pst. higher than the previous year. We actually have to go all the way back to 2010 and 2011 to find prices at this level. The Committee has therefore also pointed out in its opinion that the situation in winter 2018/2019 was exceptional and that the current action cannot therefore constitute a precedent for future situations. There is no doubt that many disadvantaged people have received an additional load associated with the electricity bills this winter. The members of the Committee are therefore pleased that the Ministry of Municipal Affairs and the Ministry of Modernisation proposes this extra payment to the households receiving housing support, and we agree with the Ministry that the housing support device is a quick and effective tool for reaching households who need additional support. When the Committee has taken the utmost care to deal with this matter as quickly as possible, it is because we want to facilitate the payment by the House Bank, which administers the housing aid scheme, to the beneficiaries concerned as soon as possible. There is in the proposition proposed an additional payment of NOK 2 950 for the first household member and NOK 120 for each additional member of the household. Overall, this measure is estimated to lead to an increase in housing aid of 274 million in 2019. kr. In addition, it's estimated that there will be about 2 million. kr in administrative expenses of the House Bank. As mentioned, the whole committee is behind the vote, but I would also like to point out that the Committee is dealing with another issue related to the same topic: Document 8:73 S for 2018-2019, on the modification of housing support and the establishment of temporary arrangements to reach disadvantaged households with high energy price problems. This is a comprehensive proposal by the Socialist Left, and it will be discussed and debated in full in plenary after Easter. We have tried to get the matter through as quickly as possible and hope that the House Bank can make payments before Easter. It is a good and unbureaucratic solution to use the House Bank as an appliance to pay the money. We hope that this will help many of them in society who have the least flexibility to deal with unforeseen expenses. ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e3523	It's time to stop tuberculosis, and Norway has a role to play here. We can make a difference, and the time is now for action. Every day 4,400 people die of tuberculosis. It is important that Norway adheres to its international commitments and contributes to the cessation of tuberculosis. We've done it before in our own country, and our history is giving us our own responsibility to take leadership in the fight. We have also committed ourselves to this through the sustainability objectives and through the Granavolden platform, where it states that Norway should be a global driver for efforts to combat antibiotic resistance. Today, resistant tuberculosis accounts for one third of all deaths related to antibiotic resistance. Increasing support for the Global Fund for the Control of AIDS, Tuberculosis and Malaria and for others working in the fight against tuberculosis, as well as strengthening the research capacity of tuberculosis medicines and vaccines can be an important step towards the goal. More than 10 million people get tuberculosis every year, and 1.6 million people die unnecessarily. The BCG vaccine is not effective enough. Antibiotic resistance, together with multi-resistance tuberculosis, is among the top ten threats to global health of the WHO in 2019. Tuberculosis has no borders, and Norway is not admirable for tuberculosis. We had almost 300 cases last year, even several multi-resistant tuberculosis infections. I want to use aid to clear up disease, just as we now use it to clear up the gardens for plastics. It's better to use aid on something concrete that we know save lives. Illness is driving many into even greater poverty and giving more migration and even more people who are linked to terrorist groups, as we see, among other things. in Sahel. The precarious health situation in the sub-Saharan area is an important driver of destabilisation, migration and terrorism. Combating tuberculosis, malaria and AIDS is therefore not just a medical intervention — it is a crucial contribution to stabilising this region. We see in the news picture that there are still major conflicts, most recently in Mali a few weeks ago, and we know that both Boko Haram and IS are active in this region. In any case, we have committed ourselves to providing a large proportion of the state budget for development and assistance. I want to make strict demands that money helps, gives people a better life and creates a better world. Last Sunday, i.e. March 24th, we marked the world's day of tuberculosis. Each year, the tuberculosis-capped day is marked with a lot of commitment, animal-acquired experience and new slogans that you might be able to get attention this time. This year's slogan: Stop the tuberculosis. But when are we really going to start listening to those who have tuberculosis? Tuberculosis is a disease that has existed for a long time. Signs of the disease have been found in mummies in Egypt and Peru. It was described in writing already 3,000 years ago, in the younger Stone Age. Unfortunately, the world’s response to this epidemic continues to be somewhat rock-old. Why is it that a completely curable bacterial infection kills 1.6 million people every year? And why is tuberculosis still one of the ten biggest threats to global health? As far as too much is concerned, the explanation lies in the lack of political will, and as far as too much is concerned, there is a lack of political will because the majority of those affected are among the poorest in poor countries and far from being part of a market that is low-buyer. When I deal with tuberculosis, it's because it's one of the diseases that also affects Norway. I might as well have talked about malaria, but we're not so afraid of malaria in Norway. But now we see that we have patients in Norway, and we see an increase. That's why it's time to research more on tuberculosis, because every year over 10 million people get this disease, of which 1 million children. Almost 40 pst. 4 million tuberculosis victims, do not have access to healthcare. They never get diagnosis or treatment, and they remain contagious until their last day. Tuberculosis is difficult to diagnose, and it is a tough treatment — the same treatment given in Norway in the 1950s. If you have resistant tuberculosis, the treatment is even more difficult, with a number of tablets and injections and a lot of side effects, and the treatment lasts a lot longer. Of well half a million people who develop resistant tuberculosis, it's just about 15 pst. which is getting healthy. Most never get diagnosis or treatment. I do not think we can accept that. We live in a time when most things are possible, but we haven't been able to do a test that can easily detect tuberculosis. We don't have enough antibiotics, and we don't have any vaccines either. Many think they're protected by BCG, but it doesn't provide full coverage. The BCG vaccine has otherwise been withdrawn from the Norwegian vaccination programme and is only given to particularly risky groups. I think we need to invest in tuberculosis. It's easy for me to stand here and say that we have to stop the epidemic, but that's what the world leaders have done, and then I can follow it up. The only world leader I know here in the Chamber is the Norwegian Minister for Development, who has to follow the ambitions of the Granavolden platform. That's why I challenge him here today. Tuberculosis control has been alarmingly underfunded for many years, all years. It's too little research. There is far too little research on drugs and vaccines against tuberculosis. With the ever-increasing development of resistance we see, tuberculosis becomes more difficult to treat. We're actually at risk of going back in time, where one didn't have any medical treatment for tuberculosis beyond breathing aid. People die every day, and for them time is up, but it is perhaps now time for Norway to take a force, as politicians say, for the fight against tuberculosis. That is why I want to challenge the Minister for Development today: the Global Fund for the Control of AIDS, Tuberculosis and Malaria will hold a filling conference this October this year. This is a good opportunity for Norway to use our anti-tuberculosis aid. Both tuberculosis and resistant tuberculosis are an increasing global threat. How does the Council of State work to follow up the Declaration of the High Level Meeting on Tuberculosis in the United Nations in 2018, and how does the Minister of Development ensure that we can be a global driver for the fight against antibiotic resistance, as it says in the Granavolden platform?
ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e3551	What many people don't know is that tuberculosis is the contagious disease that takes the most lives in the world today. Every year 10 million people develop the disease. Only six out of ten are treated. Annually, 1.6 million people die. This is 1.6 million lives lost, completely unnecessary. We have committed ourselves, as was said here, to achieving all the UN's sustainability objectives. Partial objective 3.3 is to control tuberculosis and other contagious diseases. The fight against tuberculosis is therefore central to achieving the sustainability goals. It's because it's the most vulnerable that's hit hardest. In addition, the disease afflicts households and communities for important resources. Tuberculosis is therefore not just a problem for the sick and the family; the disease also represents a major developmental problem, as the interpellant pointed out. It is gratifying to see the number of cases and deaths reduced by 2-3 pst. annual. But that's not enough. To achieve the global goals set by the world community, mortality must be reduced by 5 pst. and disease cases with 10 pst. already by 2020. In other words, the world community has to do more. Therefore, the government contributes to the escalation of international efforts against tuberculosis. The government was one of the initiators of the UN High Level Tuberculosis Summit last September. It was important to mobilise increased international efforts. The high-level meeting adopted ambitious objectives for the prevention of infection and the detection and treatment of several infected people. The fight against tuberculosis requires efforts on many fronts. I would like to mention four key areas of action. Firstly, we need to increase efforts to prevent infection. There are many areas, such as congestion, smoking, immunodeficiency, etc., that increase the risk of infection. Therefore, we will also work here to help prevent tuberculosis. Prevention is central to medical interventions. The ancient BCG vaccine is still used, as mentioned here. Through the GLOBVAC Programme of the Research Council, the Government supports several research projects for the development of a more effective vaccine. But more has to be done here too. The Unitaid International Medicines Initiative is working more effectively to promote new solutions to both prevent, diagnose and treat diseases such as tuberculosis. Through Unitaid, the government supports trials of prevention treatment for children and others who are particularly vulnerable to infection, e.g. because they live with someone who has the disease. This has proved to be so effective that it has been addressed as one of the objectives of the high-level meeting. Secondly, we need to work even more effectively to detect the disease and make the right diagnosis. Every year there are about four million undetected cases of tuberculosis. Old equipment and limited capacity in laboratories in developing countries have made it difficult to diagnose. It is particularly demanding to diagnose children correctly. Unitaid has made more diagnostic tools and more effective treatment available to both adults and children in developing countries. Countries purchase and use these tools, among other things. with funding from the Global Fund for the Control of AIDS, Tuberculosis and Malaria. Thirdly, we need to increase efforts to ensure access to effective medicines for those who need it most. Treatment of resistant tuberculosis has unfortunately taken up to two years. In addition to all this suffering, treatment also has serious side effects, such as hearing impairment and psychosis. Here, the international community has made some progress, and from 2016, the recommended duration of treatment for multi-resistant tuberculosis is down from two to less than one year. People with HIV have poor immune response and are therefore extra exposed. Twice infection with tuberculosis and HIV is a serious public health problem in many parts of the world. Through Unitaid, the government supports the trials that are, to find out more about the effects of new drugs on resistant tuberculosis. Tuberculosis treatment has also been developed for children. The results are promising. But more research is needed here – and again: especially for children. Through our partnership, the National Association for Heart and Pulmonary Disease, LHL, provides services and effective local treatment in developing countries. As one of several important contributions from the LHL, it helps patients to take the medicine correctly, and through this we avoid developing even more resistance to treatment. Fourthly, we must work harder to combat the stigmas that follow the disease. We know that tuberculosis is shameful. It infects easily, and tuberculosis patients are in many cases excluded. This makes persons infected reluctant to seek help, even where help is available. The government therefore supports the work on awareness-raising changes, information and organisation to get more people to seek such help. Experience has shown that it is absolutely necessary to bring the ones involved, to reach out here. Civil society is a very central partner to build the knowledge needed from below. The government has increased and further aims to strengthen its assistance in the fight against tuberculosis. In 2018, the government went over 150 million. kr directly to developing countries in the fight against tuberculosis. The Global Fund for the Control of AIDS, Tuberculosis and Malaria is a good partner in this work. The fund reaches disease victims in more than 100 countries. Therefore, in the three-year period that we now have behind us, from 2017 to 2019, the government will have given 2 billion. for the work of the Fund on tuberculosis, malaria and AIDS. It shall be the filling-in conference of the Fund in October 2019. Norwegian aid for the period 2020-2022 will be treated by the government later this year. My aim will then be to increase support for the fund. The World Health Organisation is the hub of international work. The government will continue to be an active co-player of the World Health Organisation in these important areas. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs also cooperates closely with the Ministry of Health and Welfare on this matter. The government is in the process of developing a strategy for development policy aimed at particularly vulnerable groups. We know that tuberculosis affects just the most vulnerable. Tuberculosis and functional impairment are interrelated. Both the disease itself and the side effects of the treatment of resistant tuberculosis may result in functional impairment as mentioned. Persons with disabilities also find it more difficult to protect themselves from infection and also have less access to health services. Therefore, we will see the response to tuberculosis in the context of the vulnerability strategy. Voluntary organisations play a particularly important role in the work of vulnerable groups, including in the fight against tuberculosis. That is why I have given Norad instructions to give the work against tuberculosis increased priority this year. This work is channelled through non-governmental organisations. Norway has long been a leading force in international work on tuberculosis and other infectious diseases. It's a role we intend to have in the continuation as well.

There's 197 million mosquito nets that work best against malaria by keeping the mosquito away. The model that The Global Fund uses is good. I've seen all the parts of it myself. ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e3615	In Norway, we also know the history of tuberculosis, and we have been in a situation that many countries worldwide are now experiencing. ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e4792	A total of eight proposals have been put forward during the debate. It's the proposals No 1-5, from Åslaug Sem-Jacobsen on behalf of the Centre Party the proposals No 6-8, from Freddy André Supreme Court on behalf of the Socialist Left Party There is a vote on the proposals Nos. 6-8, from the Socialist Left Party. Proposal No. 6 sounds: ‘The government is asked to draw up a four-year escalation plan with earmarked funds to increase the staffing of municipal child protection in proposals for the State budget for 2020.’ Proposal No. 7 sounds: ‘The government is asking for the introduction of an advisory staffing standard in which a case manager with an administrative function in municipal child protection cannot be responsible for more than a maximum of 9 to 15 children.’ [[function 244]] Proposal No. 8 sounds: ‘The government asks the government to establish a lower staffing limit for the number of municipal child protection posts and to return to the Stortinge in an appropriate manner by 2020.’ The Greens and Reds have announced support for the proposals.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e4830	There is a vote on proposal No. 2nd from the Center Party. The proposal reads as follows: ‘The Government asks the Government to promote a new model of child protection management in which the statutory tasks of the municipalities are continued and strengthened, in which Bufetat’s regional tasks are transferred to the county councils, where Buffdir is laid down and the Ministry of Children and Gender Equality is given overall responsibility as legislator, budget officer and facilitating control and supervisory tasks, and where the role of the county council as control and supervisory authority is strengthened.’
ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e4845	There is a vote on proposal No. Three, from the Center Party. The proposal reads as follows: ‘The government asks the government to ensure that all children and young people assessed in foster homes/institutions receive, as early as possible, a health evaluation/consultation to ensure the monitoring, prevention and early follow-up of any emotional or behavioural problems and mental difficulties or suffering.’ Red has announced support for the proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e4862	There is a vote on the proposals Nos. Four and five from the Center Party. Proposal No. 4 sounds: ‘The government is asking for a case to be brought before the government to legislate that the child’s needs and mental health should always be carefully monitored before being placed in foster homes or in a child protection institution.’ Proposal No. 5 sounds: ‘The government is asking for a case to be brought by the government to legislate cooperation agreements between child protection, health institutions and municipalities with content requirements, including procedures for cross-sectoral locations and dispute resolution arrangements.’ [[rust and psychiatry]] The Socialist Left has announced support for the proposals.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e4886	There is a vote on proposal No. One from the Center Party. The proposal reads as follows: ‘The government is asked to draw up a four-year escalation plan with the aim of increasing the staffing of municipal child protection.’ The Socialist Left and Red have announced subsidiary support for the proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-04-02.ana.ud170e4911	The Socialist Left Party, the Green Party and the Red Party have announced that they will vote against it.

The proposal reads as follows: ‘I In Law No 24 March 2000 16 on occupational retirement provision is amended as follows: Paragraphs 3 to 3 of the first paragraph shall be replaced by the following: the pension scheme shall cover all employees of the undertaking who are at least 13 years of age, unless otherwise provided for in the law here or in the regulations laid down by the King. [[1]] Paragraphs 3 to 5 are replaced by the A part-time employee's earned pension shall be a proportionate part of the pension that would have been earned if the employee had a full-time job. II In Law No. 24 November 2000 No. 81 on occupational retirement provision is amended as follows: Paragraphs 4 to 2 of the first paragraph shall be replaced by the following: the pension scheme shall cover all employees of the undertaking who have reached the age of 13 years, unless otherwise provided for in the law here or in the regulations laid down by the King. [[1]] The legislation may provide that workers who have reached the age of 75 shall not be admitted as members. the third paragraph is deleted; The current fourth term is going to be the third term. Section 5-5, first paragraph, the second sentence is replaced by the following: For the calculation of wages and salaries, pay from the first crown shall be used. The current second sentence is going to be a new third sentence. III In Law 13 December 2013 No. 106 on occupational retirement provision is amended as follows: Paragraphs 3 to 2 of the first paragraph shall be replaced by the following: the pension scheme shall cover all employees of the undertaking who are at least 13 years of age. [[1]] The king may derogate from the provision of the first sentence by way of regulation. Paragraphs 3-4 are replaced by the following: The part-time employee's retirement benefit shall be a proportionate part of the pension that would have been earned if the employee had a full-time job. IV The amendments shall enter into force on 1 January 2021.’;»
